People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I don’t know how much my weight has changed exactly, but my appetite is definitely off, if that’s helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these feelings of sadness and emptiness for most days over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been there most days.\n\nIt’s been about a month now, really — since my sister died so unexpectedly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I almost didn’t come in, because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. But lately there have been mornings where I wake up and think… it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I just… that thought scared me enough that I thought I should talk to someone. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died unexpectedly… I don’t mean that every single minute has been like that, but that’s when those thoughts began showing up. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I still go to things. I don’t want to just disappear on people, so I make myself go to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve kept showing up for.\n\nBut lately it’s like I’m there and not really there, if that makes sense. Like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else enjoy it. Even being in my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors, which usually gives me a little lift… it just doesn’t feel the same right now. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of energy or fatigue since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I keep telling myself it’s just normal, you know, after everything.\n\nBut even small things have been taking it out of me. Running a simple errand, or getting papers together for the Cultural Center… things I used to do without thinking… I’ll finish and feel completely worn out, like I’m moving through thick mud. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say consistently, no… I mean, of course I’ve been sad, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. I do have stretches where I’m just sort of quiet or hollow, but then I’ll distract myself or be around people and it’s not as noticeable. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, I think… I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. It’s more that food just doesn’t seem to call to me the way it used to.\n\nI’ll make myself something small, like toast or a bit of soup, and then later in the evening I’ll realize I barely ate much at all. I don’t know how much my weight has changed exactly, but my appetite is definitely off, if that’s helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these feelings of sadness and emptiness for most days over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day… yes, I think so. I don’t want to worry anyone, and I’m not planning to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut what scared me enough to ask for help is that I keep having mornings where I think it might just be easier if I didn’t wake up. That sounds terrible to say out loud… but that’s what it’s been like.\n\nDoctor: For how many months have you been feeling this way since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: About a month, I’d say… since my sister died so unexpectedly. I don’t want to just disappear on people, so I make myself go to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve kept showing up for.\n\nBut lately it’s like I’m there and not really there, if that makes sense. Like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else enjoy it. Even being in my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors, which usually gives me a little lift… it just doesn’t feel the same right now. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of energy or fatigue since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I keep telling myself it’s just normal, you know, after everything.\n\nBut even small things have been taking it out of me. Running a simple errand, or getting papers together for the Cultural Center… things I used to do without thinking… I’ll finish and feel completely worn out, like I’m moving through thick mud. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say consistently, no… I mean, of course I’ve been sad, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. I do have stretches where I’m just sort of quiet or hollow, but then I’ll distract myself or be around people and it’s not as noticeable. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, I think… I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. It’s more that food just doesn’t seem to call to me the way it used to.\n\nI’ll make myself something small, like toast or a bit of soup, and then later in the evening I’ll realize I barely ate much at all. I don’t know how much my weight has changed exactly, but my appetite is definitely off, if that’s helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these feelings of sadness and emptiness for most days over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been there most days.\n\nIt’s been about a month now, really — since my sister died so unexpectedly. Some days are better than others, but those sad, empty feelings have been coming up pretty regularly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day… yes, I think so. I don’t want to worry anyone, and I’m not planning to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut what scared me enough to ask for help is that I keep having mornings where I think it might just be easier if I didn’t wake up. That sounds terrible to say out loud… but that’s what it’s been like.\n\nDoctor: For how many months have you been feeling this way since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: About a month, I’d say… since my sister died so unexpectedly. It feels like maybe I should be handling it better by now, but that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still trying to keep up with things. I still go to book club, and sometimes the dance night, because I don’t want to just sit at home.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, if that makes sense. Even my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors — things that used to give me a nice little lift — they just don’t seem to do that the same way right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but now find you don't enjoy at all?\n\nPatient: Not completely “at all,” maybe… I mean, I do still make myself go to book club and sometimes the dance night, because I don’t want to stop everything.\n\nBut I don’t seem to enjoy them the way I did. I’m there, but it’s like I’m behind a pane of glass. And even my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift, and lately they just… don’t, really. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than two months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two months… it’s been about a month, really, since my sister died so suddenly. I hope I’m answering that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in how you've been feeling since these thoughts and feelings started about a month ago?\n\nPatient: Not a big improvement, no… I mean, I’ve had a few slightly easier moments, I suppose. But overall it’s still been going on since my sister died so unexpectedly, about a month ago. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but now find you have no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t want to say I have no pleasure in anything at all. I still do make myself go to book club, and sometimes the dance night, because I know it’s probably good for me.\n\nBut it feels different lately. I’m sort of sitting there like I’m behind a pane of glass, not really part of it. And even things like my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors… they used to give me a little lift, and now they just don’t seem to. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Running a simple errand, or getting papers together for the Cultural Center… things I used to do without thinking… I’ll finish and feel completely worn out, like I’m moving through thick mud. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say consistently, no… I mean, of course I’ve been sad, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. I do have stretches where I’m just sort of quiet or hollow, but then I’ll distract myself or be around people and it’s not as noticeable. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, I think… I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. It’s more that food just doesn’t seem to call to me the way it used to.\n\nI’ll make myself something small, like toast or a bit of soup, and then later in the evening I’ll realize I barely ate much at all. I don’t know how much my weight has changed exactly, but my appetite is definitely off, if that’s helpful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these feelings of sadness and emptiness for most days over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been there most days.\n\nIt’s been about a month now, really — since my sister died so unexpectedly. Some days are better than others, but those sad, empty feelings have been coming up pretty regularly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day… yes, I think so. I don’t want to worry anyone, and I’m not planning to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut what scared me enough to ask for help is that I keep having mornings where I think it might just be easier if I didn’t wake up. That sounds terrible to say out loud… but that’s what it’s been like.\n\nDoctor: For how many months have you been feeling this way since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: About a month, I’d say… since my sister died so unexpectedly. It feels like maybe I should be handling it better by now, but that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still trying to keep up with things. I still go to book club, and sometimes the dance night, because I don’t want to just sit at home.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, if that makes sense. Even my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors — things that used to give me a nice little lift — they just don’t seem to do that the same way right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but now find you don't enjoy at all?\n\nPatient: Not completely “at all,” maybe… I mean, I do still make myself go to book club and sometimes the dance night, because I don’t want to stop everything.\n\nBut I don’t seem to enjoy them the way I did. I’m there, but it’s like I’m behind a pane of glass. And even my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift, and lately they just… don’t, really. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than two months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two months… it’s been about a month, really, since my sister died so suddenly. I hope I’m answering that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in how you've been feeling since these thoughts and feelings started about a month ago?\n\nPatient: Not a big improvement, no… I mean, I’ve had a few slightly easier moments, I suppose. But overall it’s still been going on since my sister died so unexpectedly, about a month ago. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but now find you have no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t want to say I have no pleasure in anything at all. I still do make myself go to book club, and sometimes the dance night, because I know it’s probably good for me.\n\nBut it feels different lately. I’m sort of sitting there like I’m behind a pane of glass, not really part of it. And even things like my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors… they used to give me a little lift, and now they just don’t seem to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for less than two months?\n\nPatient: Yes, less than two months. It’s been about a month now, since my sister died so unexpectedly… if that’s the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but now find you have no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: I think I may have said this a little already, but… yes, in a way. I still make myself go to book club or the dance night, so it’s not as if I’ve completely stopped doing things. But lately I feel sort of separate when I’m there, like I’m behind a pane of glass.\n\nAnd things I used to get a bit of joy from, like my garden or cooking something for the neighbors… they just don’t give me that little lift anymore. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I almost didn’t come in, because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It started after my sister died unexpectedly… I don’t mean that every single minute has been like that, but that’s when those thoughts began showing up. I don’t want to just disappear on people, so I make myself go to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve kept showing up for.\n\nBut lately it’s like I’m there and not really there, if that makes sense. Like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else enjoy it. Even being in my garden, or cooking something for the neighbors, which usually gives me a little lift… it just doesn’t feel the same right now. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of energy or fatigue since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I keep telling myself it’s just normal, you know, after everything.\n\nBut even small things have been taking it out of me. Running a simple errand, or getting papers together for the Cultural Center… things I used to do without thinking… I’ll finish and feel completely worn out, like I’m moving through thick mud. I don’t know if that counts as significant, but it does feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say consistently, no… I mean, of course I’ve been sad, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. I do have stretches where I’m just sort of quiet or hollow, but then I’ll distract myself or be around people and it’s not as noticeable. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, I think… I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. It’s more that food just doesn’t seem to call to me the way it used to.\n\nI’ll make myself something small, like toast or a bit of soup, and then later in the evening I’ll realize I barely ate much at all.
